## Management Summary

### Verdict

🔴 **Critical security concerns** - This is an intentionally vulnerable web shop where an anonymous attacker can log in as an administrator, read customer data and run code on the server. Acceptable only as an isolated training target; it must never hold real data or be reachable from untrusted networks.

**Risk distribution:** 🔴 Critical: 3 · 🟠 High: 32 · 🟡 Medium: 46 · 🟢 Low: n/a · **Total: 81**<br/>**Findings register:** 154 · 🔴 Critical: 5 · 🟠 High: 59 · 🟡 Medium: 90 - the distribution above counts insecure-practice findings under their weakness; see [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register)<br/>**Assessment evidence:** 56 of 79 finding(s) confirmed in code<br/>**Weakness classes:** 51 (44 implementation, 7 design) - see [§7 Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register)

<br/>

**Security concerns behind this assessment:**

<blockquote style="border-left: 3px solid #dc2626; padding: 16px 20px; margin: 0;">

- **Login bypass and customer data theft** — SQL injection in product search and login lets an unauthenticated visitor read the user table and sign in as the administrator. *(🔴 [F-002](#f-002), 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) → [W-001](#w-001))*
- **Server takeover through stored profile text** — A signed-in user can store a crafted username that the server executes as code when the profile page loads. *(🔴 [F-010](#f-010) → [W-007](#w-007))* — left open by the evidence review: 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Server-side eval of stored username on profile render
- **Other customers' records read and changed** — Missing ownership checks (IDOR) let any signed-in customer read or modify another customer's baskets, orders and reviews. *(🔴 [F-008](#f-008) → [W-005](#w-005))* — left open by the evidence review: 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — Insecure Direct Object Reference
- **Forged identities for any account** — The token signing key is committed to the source repository and tokens are verified weakly, so anyone with the key can impersonate any user. *(🟠 [F-019](#f-019), 🟡 [F-084](#f-084) → [W-011](#w-011), [W-003](#w-003))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain
- **Anyone can register as administrator** — Mass assignment on account registration accepts a role field from the request, so an unauthenticated visitor creates an administrator account. *(🔴 [F-011](#f-011), 🟠 [F-075](#f-075), 🟠 [F-076](#f-076) → [W-002](#w-002))* — ✓ cited finding in a code-verified chain

</blockquote>

<br/>

Real customer data, payments or internet exposure would put accounts and the server at direct risk until these weaknesses are fixed.

**About this assessment:** An AI-assisted threat model derived from the implementation of Juice Shop. It reconstructs the implemented architecture (9 components and 5 external services, see [§2](#2-architecture-diagrams)) and identifies threats and control gaps in it.


**Method and limits:** Automated static analysis of code and configuration, not a pentest or a team threat-modeling session; Low and Informational findings not reported (threshold: medium) - see [§11 Out of Scope](#11-out-of-scope).

### Remediation Priorities

The root causes behind the findings, ranked by severity, each with the measures to do first. The lasting fix under a root cause removes it and every finding it causes. Every mitigation that fixes a Critical finding is listed. Details in the [§7 Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register) and the [§10 Mitigation Register](#10-mitigation-register).

### Open Questions for the Team

The code cannot answer these questions; the people who own the business and the deployment can. Each question states under it what its answer changes.

- _How critical are User credentials and session tokens and Application records in SQLite to the business, and what harm would their disclosure, manipulation or unavailability cause?_
  The answer weights the impact rating and fix order of every finding that reaches these assets.
- _If an attacker runs code in Juice Shop Express API, which other services, secrets or credentials can that process reach?_ ([F-010](#f-010) (unproven), [F-060](#f-060) (unproven) +1 more)
  Every reachable service or credential extends this from one component to a wider compromise and raises the priority of isolating that process; an isolated process contains it.
- _Which sensitive business records can the production database account used by Juice Shop Express API or Authentication & Session Surface access?_ ([W-001](#w-001): [F-002](#f-002), [F-009](#f-009))
  Personal or regulated data in reach raises both the breach impact and any notification duty; a narrowly scoped account limits the blast radius.

### Security Posture & Top Threats

**Figure 1a - Runtime Architecture and Threat Overview**

At runtime, Juice Shop has 8 components in 3 layers and exchanges data with 5 external services. Figure 1a shows these data flows and where each of the 7 attack scenarios below begins. The complete boundary catalogue remains in [§1 Trust Boundaries](#trust-boundaries).

<picture><source media="(prefers-color-scheme: dark)" srcset="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure1-dark.svg"><img src="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure1.svg" alt="Figure 1a - Runtime Architecture and Threat Overview"></picture>

**Build pipeline**, not drawn in this runtime view: GitHub Actions, GitLab CI. ⑧ A2 · Supply-Chain / Build Attacker → [Figure 1b](#figure-1b).

Role access and finding assignments are listed in [Identified Actors](#identified-actors).

**Actor grouping.** Regular self-registered users are grouped with anonymous internet attackers because registration is open. Each finding retains its login and privilege requirements.

<a id="figure-1b"></a>

**Figure 1b - Supply Chain and Build**

Juice Shop builds in GitHub Actions, GitLab CI. Figure 1b shows the inputs, build systems and release artifacts the repository evidences, with 9 findings attached to them. The highlighted path follows 🟠 [F-027](#f-027) — Dependency lockfile generation disabled by npm configuration — .npmrc:1 as far as the repository evidences it. The control assessment is in [§6.11](#611-operations-runtime-and-supply-chain-controls).

<picture><source media="(prefers-color-scheme: dark)" srcset="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure1b-dark.svg"><img src="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure1b.svg" alt="Figure 1b - Supply Chain and Build"></picture>

**Figure 2 - Attack Routes and Impact**

Each numbered route names one example finding from the corresponding Top Threats group with its access prerequisite, the register weakness it is linked to, and the group's potential business harm, which depends on deployment and affected assets. W-IDs in parentheses link the example to an existing weakness. The attack step, affected component and technical consequence are listed in the findings register. Red arrows indicate attacks; dashed red arrows involve a victim; grey arrows lead to potential harm.

<picture><source media="(prefers-color-scheme: dark)" srcset="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure2-dark.svg"><img src="security-assessment-juice-shop-thorough-v0.1.0.figure2.svg" alt="Figure 2 - Actors, attack routes, weaknesses and impact"></picture>

**Threat actors.** The actors below drive the numbered attack paths in the figures above. The **Juice Shop User** is the *victim* of client-side attacks (XSS / CSRF), not an attacker. Figure 2 marks victim interaction on the applicable attack routes.

- **Juice Shop User** — legitimate end user; target of client-side attacks; target of ⑥ Output Encoding / Cross-Site Scripting.
- **Internet Attacker** — can self-register a regular account; drives ① Broken Authorization & Access Control, ② Insecure Query Construction & Data Access, ③ Remote Code Execution (unsafe eval), ④ Hardcoded Secrets & Weak Cryptography, ⑤ Sensitive File & Secret Exposure, ⑥ Output Encoding / Cross-Site Scripting, ⑦ CSRF / Permissive CORS.
- **Supply-Chain / Build Attacker** — compromises a dependency or build pipeline, or contributes malicious code to a public repo; drives ⑧ Build Pipeline & Dependency Integrity.

**8 structural threats**, grouped by weakness class - each row is one threat, not one finding. *Threat Description* states the general architectural weakness (STRIDE in brackets); *Findings* lists the concrete instances by severity under their component, each linked to the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register), and on screen an ID shows its title when you point at it; *Risk & Impact* combines severity with business consequence; *Primary Measures* names the most important measures for this threat, most important first, one per root cause. All measures are in the [§10 Mitigation Register](#10-mitigation-register).

| <a id="path-cross-site-request-forgery"></a>⑦ | **CSRF / Permissive CORS** _(S·T)_<br/>a permissive CORS policy plus missing anti-CSRF tokens let any external page issue authenticated state-changing requests in the victim's session. | → [C-08](#c-08) Angular Frontend<br/>🟠&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-017" title="Google sign-in uses implicit flow without state">F-017</a></span><br/>→ [C-03](#c-03) Juice Shop Express API<br/>🟡&nbsp;<span style="white-space:nowrap"><a href="#f-106" title="Cookie-authenticated profile update">F-106</a></span> | 🟠 High<br/>Customer Session Hijack | <span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-176](#m-176)</span> - Add anti-CSRF protection to state-changing requests |

_STRIDE: S spoofing · T tampering · R repudiation · I information disclosure · D denial of service · E elevation of privilege. Risk, findings, components, impact and primary measures are derived deterministically; only the one-line weakness description is authored._

**Verified attack chains.** 12 fully viable ([AC-B-002](#ac-b-002), [AC-T-004](#ac-t-004), [AC-B-001](#ac-b-001), [AC-B-003](#ac-b-003), [AC-T-006](#ac-t-006), [AC-T-007](#ac-t-007), [AC-B-005](#ac-b-005), [AC-B-007](#ac-b-007), [AC-T-005](#ac-t-005), [AC-T-003](#ac-t-003), [MODEL-AC-002](#model-ac-002), [MODEL-AC-001](#model-ac-001)); 1 partially blocked ([AC-T-002](#ac-t-002)). These chains combine individual findings into end-to-end exploitation paths verified step-by-step against the code - see [§9 Abuse Cases](#9-abuse-cases) for the per-step breakdown and blocking mitigations.

### AI / LLM Exposure

This system embeds an LLM/AI surface (an LLM/AI integration); the risks below are architectural - they follow from how untrusted input reaches the model's prompt, tools, and outputs. See **[§6 Security Architecture](#6-security-architecture)** for the per-control detail.



- **[ASI03](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/) Agent Identity & Privilege Abuse** — The agent can act with an over-broad or inherited identity instead of scoped delegation. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — Chat identity taken from JWT decoded without signature

- **[LLM01](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01/) Prompt Injection** — Untrusted user input reaches the LLM prompt/context without sufficient trust separation, letting an attacker override system instructions, redirect tool calls, or coerce unintended model behaviour. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-059](#f-059) — Model-selected coupon tool enforces discount policy only _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/), [ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-073](#f-073) — Chatbot generateCoupon tool issues arbitrary discounts _([ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/), [ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-078](#f-078) — Prompt injection makes chatbot issue coupons with arbitrary
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-114](#f-114) — Stored user-written reviews returned as tool results _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/), [ASI06](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-115](#f-115) — Caller-supplied chat history and roles passed unvalidated to the model _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_

- **[LLM06](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm06/) Excessive Agency** — The LLM is granted tool/function-calling authority without a secondary authorization boundary, so a manipulated model turn can invoke privileged actions on the user's behalf. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-059](#f-059) — Model-selected coupon tool enforces discount policy only _([ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/), [ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-073](#f-073) — Chatbot generateCoupon tool issues arbitrary discounts _([ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/), [ASI01](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-078](#f-078) — Prompt injection makes chatbot issue coupons with arbitrary
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-083](#f-083) — Chat identity taken from JWT decoded without signature _([ASI03](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-163](#f-163) — Chatbot order lookup binds ownership by lossy _([ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_

- **[LLM07](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm07/) System Prompt Leakage** — The system prompt — carrying internal policy, tool rules, or secrets — is extractable through conversational manipulation, exposing the tool capability surface and internal business logic encoded in it. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API)_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-125](#f-125) — Confidential discount policy in system prompt can

- **[LLM08](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm08/) Vector and Embedding Weaknesses** — Retrieval/embedding inputs are not isolated by trust, letting injected documents or queries steer retrieval-augmented responses or leak indexed content. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API)_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-163](#f-163) — Chatbot order lookup binds ownership by lossy _([ASI02](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_

- **[LLM10](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm10/) Unbounded Consumption** — The LLM endpoint imposes no authentication, rate, or quota boundary, so any client can drive unbounded model invocations — uncontrolled provider cost and denial of service for legitimate users. _([C-03](#c-03) — Juice Shop Express API)_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-154](#f-154) — Unbounded LLM consumption on unauthenticated chat endpoint _([ASI08](https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/))_

### Operational Strengths

Operational controls rated Adequate or Partial - grouped into broad clusters (full per-control breakdown in [§6](#6-security-architecture)). Clusters demoted to Weak by open Critical/High findings appear in [§6](#6-security-architecture) instead, not here.

<table style="table-layout:fixed;width:100%;max-width:1240px">
<colgroup><col width="18%" style="width:18%"><col width="32%" style="width:32%"><col width="14%" style="width:14%"><col width="36%" style="width:36%"></colgroup>
<thead><tr><th>Strength</th><th>What's in Place</th><th>Effectiveness</th><th>Open gaps</th></tr></thead>
<tbody>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Container &amp; Supply-Chain Hardening</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Build-time and runtime hardening - minimal base image, non-root execution, dependency inventory.</em><br/>Static analysis (CodeQL) and SBOM generation<br/>Container image hardening<br/>Dependency lockfile and install integrity</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">✅ Adequate</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">-</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Hardened HTTP Stack</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Browser-facing HTTP hardening — security headers, cookie flags, cross-origin policy, and abuse-protection limits.</em><br/>WebSocket origin restriction (CORS)<br/>Rate limiting</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">⚠️ Partial</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">1 High and 1 Medium findings remain open:<br/>🟠 <a href="#f-017">F-017</a> — Google sign-in uses implicit flow without state<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-106">F-106</a> — Cookie-authenticated profile update</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Observability &amp; Audit</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Runtime visibility - access logging, audit trails, and operational telemetry for post-incident review.</em><br/>HTTP access logging<br/>Error handling and logging for WebSocket listeners<br/>Sequelize ORM database connection configuration</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">⚠️ Partial</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">-</td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>


**Bottom line:** These controls narrow specific attack surfaces but none eliminates a Critical finding on its own.

## Critical Findings by Root Cause

5 of 5 Critical findings trace back to 4 root causes; fixing a root cause addresses every finding linked to it.

```mermaid

graph LR
    GOAL["5 Critical findings"]:::goal
    W001["W-001<br/>Database access relies on concatenated queries"]:::rootcause
    T002["F-002 - SQL injection in product search query<br/>search.ts"]:::leaf
    T009["F-009 - SQL injection in login query enables authentication bypass<br/>login.ts"]:::leaf
    W002["W-002<br/>Privileged attributes lack an enforced write allowlist"]:::rootcause
    T011["F-011 - Mass assignment of admin role during user registration<br/>server.ts"]:::leaf
    W005["W-005<br/>Authorization is implemented route by route"]:::rootcause
    T008["F-008 - Insecure Direct Object Reference<br/>payment.ts"]:::leaf
    W007["W-007<br/>Application data crosses into executable code"]:::rootcause
    T010["F-010 - Server-side eval of stored username on profile render<br/>userProfile.ts"]:::leaf
```

**Structural fixes** (full detail in [§7 Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register)):

- **[W-001](#w-001)** — Provide one parameterised repository or query-builder path and prohibit application-value interpolation in database queries _(closes [F-002](#f-002), [F-009](#f-009))_
- **[W-002](#w-002)** — Define operation-specific write schemas and assign privileged attributes exclusively through server-side policy _(closes [F-011](#f-011))_
- **[W-005](#w-005)** — Enforce authorization through a shared server-side policy layer and make ownership and tenant scope mandatory inputs to data access _(closes [F-008](#f-008))_
- **[W-007](#w-007)** — Replace evaluation of application values with a data parser or a fixed set of operations whose arguments remain data _(closes [F-010](#f-010))_

## 1. System Overview

Probably the most modern and sophisticated insecure web application

**Repository:** `https://github.com/juice-shop/juice-shop.git`
**Runtime:** Node\.js 22 - 26

### Scope

This threat model covers 9 components of juice-shop: 

- Sequelize application data access
- CI/CD Pipeline
- Juice Shop Express API
- Web3 / Wallet / NFT Surface
- marsdb Embedded Store
- Real-time WebSocket Channel
- SQLite Embedded Store
- Angular Frontend
- Authentication & Session Surface

**Not individually examined:** 1 source file in 1 component had no route, lead or security signal (CI/CD Pipeline 1).

**Not analysed:** 92 TypeScript files the build leaves out, so their routes and code sites were not checked (`data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_1_correct.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_2.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_3.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/accessLogDisclosureChallenge_4.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_1_correct.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_2.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_3.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/adminSectionChallenge_4.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/changeProductChallenge_1.ts`, `data/static/codefixes/changeProductChallenge_2.ts` and 82 more).

**Out of scope:** third-party hosted dependencies, browser runtime, operating-system kernel, and the underlying network infrastructure.

**Basis:** An automated, AI-assisted threat model built from the repository's source code and configuration - static analysis only, no dynamic or penetration testing. It describes the system as built, not as designed.

### Identified Actors

Figure 1a draws **Internet Attacker** as attacker and **Juice Shop User** and **Juice Shop Admin** as legitimate roles of Juice Shop. [Figure 1b](#figure-1b) draws **Supply-Chain / Build Attacker** and the build it attacks. Each row gives that actor's access and the findings attributed to it.

| Actor | Type | Access | Findings |
|----------------------|---------------|----------------------|-----------------|
| A1 · Internet Attacker | Attacker | Can self-register a regular account | 🔴 5 · 🟠 58 · 🟡 76 |
| A2 · Supply-Chain / Build Attacker | Attacker | Compromises a dependency or build pipeline,<br/>or contributes malicious code to a public<br/>repo | 🟠 1 · 🟡 8 |
| Juice Shop User | Role (victim) | Person using the client application,<br/>evidenced by its browser entry point. | - |
| Juice Shop Admin | Privileged role | Application role with elevated rights. | - |

6 findings (6 Medium) have no assigned actor. That does not mean they are out of reach.

### Trust Boundaries

Each row names a crossing and the condition it relies on. A modelled crossing does not prove that its control works.

<tbody>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><a id="tb-1"></a>tb-1 · External → Juice Shop Express API</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">Every state-changing or user-specific route is registered behind <code>security.<wbr>isAuthorized()</code> before its handler runs.</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><code>security.<wbr>isAuthorized()</code> route middleware</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Gap:</strong> <em>Validation</em><br/>🔴 <a href="#f-002">F-002</a> — SQL injection in product search query<br/><em>Authorization</em><br/>🟠 <a href="#f-001">F-001</a> — Missing object ownership checks on basket, basket item, checkout and review<br/>🟠 <a href="#f-062">F-062</a> — Any authenticated user can read any user record by id<br/>🟠 <a href="#f-072">F-072</a> — Unauthenticated callers can modify any product including its<br/>🟡 <a href="#f-108">F-108</a> — Duplicate JSON keys bypass basket ownership check</td></tr>

A control counts for the component whose paths hold its implementation evidence. A finding counts for every component it affects, so the columns can add up to more than the register. [§6](#6-security-architecture) holds the full assessment.*

> **Legend:** `-->` synchronous request/response (REST, HTTPS, gRPC) · **red border** ≥ 3 Critical threats on the component · **amber border** ≥ 2 High threats

## 3. Attack Walkthroughs

This section walks through how the highest-risk findings are exploited - one short walkthrough per Critical, each with attack steps, a focused sequence diagram, and the primary mitigation. Which root cause each Critical traces back to is in [Critical Findings by Root Cause](#critical-findings-by-root-cause); full per-finding context (severity rationale, assets, detection signals) is in the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register).

### 3.1 Mass assignment of admin role during user registration

**Source:** 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — `server.ts:501`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)). STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege. See [§8 F-011](#f-011) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I POST `/api/Users` with email, password, `passwordRepeat` and `role=admin` and no authentication.
2. The finale create stores my role as admin, and I log in to receive an admin JWT.
3. I use the admin token to access administration functions and other users' data.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express API - server.ts:501
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting server.ts line 501
    App->>App: Mass assignment of admin role during user registration
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-915
    alt Current state — F-011
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-011
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-172 — Allowlist registration fields and force customer role
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-172](#m-172) (Allowlist registration fields and force customer role) lands, 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Mass assignment of admin role during user registration is exploitable at `server.ts:501` (Critical-severity, [CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-172](#m-172) (Allowlist registration fields and force customer role)

### 3.2 Insecure Direct Object Reference (Card) in Payment

**Source:** 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — `routes/payment.ts:21`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-008](#f-008) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker crafts a request that reaches the code at `routes/payment.ts:21`.
2. A signed-in attacker puts another user's ID into the owner field of the request (`req.body.UserId` and the like); the query trusts it instead of the session identity, so the attacker reads or changes every other user's records - horizontal privilege escalation across all accounts.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express API - routes/payment.ts:21
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/payment.ts line 21
    App->>App: Insecure Direct Object Reference
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-639
    alt Current state — F-008
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-008
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-169 — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (F-008)
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-169](#m-169) (Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (🔴 [F-008](#f-008))) lands, 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — Insecure Direct Object Reference is exploitable at `routes/payment.ts:21` (Critical-severity, [CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-169](#m-169) (Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (🔴 [F-008](#f-008)))
- Defence in depth: ◑ [M-152](#m-152) (Manual review: verify Insecure Direct Object Reference (Card))

### 3.3 SQL injection in product search query

**Source:** 🔴 [F-002](#f-002) — `routes/search.ts:23`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-002](#f-002) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I call GET `/rest/products/search` without logging in, putting a quote-terminated UNION SELECT on the Users table in q.
2. The server concatenates my q into the raw query and returns Users rows as product JSON.
3. I crack or reuse the returned password hashes to log in as administrators.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: Juice Shop Express API - routes/search.ts:23
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: GET /rest/products/search with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-002
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-002
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-168 — Use parameterized replacements for search criteria
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-168](#m-168) (Use parameterized replacements for search criteria) lands, 🔴 [F-002](#f-002) — SQL injection in product search query is exploitable at `routes/search.ts:23` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-168](#m-168) (Use parameterized replacements for search criteria)

### 3.4 SQL injection in login query enables authentication bypass

**Source:** 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — `routes/login.ts:34`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-009](#f-009) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I send POST `/rest/user/login` with email "' OR 1=1--" and any password, needing no prior account.
2. The raw SQL drops the password check and returns the first user row, typically the admin.
3. I receive a signed `RS256` JWT for that account and use it as a bearer token on admin APIs.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: Authentication & Session Surface - routes/login.ts:34
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: POST /rest/user/login with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-009
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-009
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-170 — Use parameterized queries for login lookup
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-170](#m-170) (Use parameterized queries for login lookup) lands, 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — SQL injection in login query enables authentication bypass is exploitable at `routes/login.ts:34` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-170](#m-170) (Use parameterized queries for login lookup)

### 3.5 Server-side eval of stored username on profile render

**Source:** 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — `routes/userProfile.ts:61`

Severity 🔴 Critical ([CWE-95](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/95.html)). STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege. See [§8 F-010](#f-010) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. I register an account and log in to get a token cookie.
2. I POST `/profile` with username=#{`global.process.mainModule.require('child_process').execSync('id')`}.
3. I GET `/profile` and the server evals my username, running my command as the Node process.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express API - routes/userProfile.ts:61
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/userProfile.ts line 61
    App->>App: Server-side eval of stored username on profile render
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-95
    alt Current state — F-010
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-010
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-171 — Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input (F-010)
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-171](#m-171) (Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input (🔴 [F-010](#f-010))) lands, 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Server-side eval of stored username on profile render is exploitable at `routes/userProfile.ts:61` (Critical-severity, [CWE-95](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/95.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-171](#m-171) (Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input (🔴 [F-010](#f-010)))
- Defence in depth: ◑ [M-153](#m-153) (Manual review: verify Server-side eval of stored username on profile render)

### 3.6 JWT RSA private signing key embedded in source code

**Source:** 🟠 [F-019](#f-019) — `lib/insecurity.ts:21`

Severity 🟠 High ([CWE-321](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/321.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-019](#f-019) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. The `RS256` private key used to sign JWTs is a literal at `lib/insecurity.ts:21`.
2. Anyone who can read the repository or a deployed bundle obtains it and can sign arbitrary tokens, for example ones claiming the admin role or another user's id.
3. They then send such a token as a Bearer token to routes protected by `isAuthorized` (line 52), and `isAuthorized()` and `verify()` accept it as genuine.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant Repo
    participant App
    Note over Repo: Public repository
    Note over App: Authentication & Session Surface - lib/insecurity.ts:21
    Attacker->>Repo: Clone or browse - extract private key from lib/insecurity.ts line 21
    Attacker->>Attacker: Sign forged token / signature with the extracted key
    Attacker->>App: Submit forged artefact in normal authentication flow
    App-->>Attacker: Request accepted as authentic - signature validates
    alt Current state — F-019
        Attacker->>Repo: The attacker sends the exploit for F-019
        Repo-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-178 — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store
        Attacker->>Repo: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        Repo-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-178](#m-178) (Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store) lands, 🟠 [F-019](#f-019) — JWT RSA private signing key embedded in source code is exploitable at `lib/insecurity.ts:21` (High-severity, [CWE-321](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/321.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-178](#m-178) (Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store)

### 3.7 User registration accepts privileged role from request body

**Source:** 🟠 [F-075](#f-075) — `server.ts:408`

Severity 🟠 High ([CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)). STRIDE: Elevation of Privilege. See [§8 F-075](#f-075) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Attacker sends POST `/api/Users` with JSON body including role set to admin and chosen credentials.
2. Pre-handlers at `server.ts:408-422` only trim fields and detect the attempt, then call `next()`.
3. The finale User resource persists the role, which the model's `isIn` list allows.
4. Attacker logs in and receives a JWT carrying role admin, which grants admin-only functions.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express API - server.ts:408
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting server.ts line 408
    App->>App: User registration accepts privileged role from request body
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-915
    alt Current state — F-075
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-075
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-222 — Strip privileged fields from user registration
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-222](#m-222) (Strip privileged fields from user registration) lands, 🟠 [F-075](#f-075) — User registration accepts privileged role from request body is exploitable at `server.ts:408` (High-severity, [CWE-915](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/915.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-222](#m-222) (Strip privileged fields from user registration)

### 3.8 JWT verification accepts token-chosen algorithm with public key

**Source:** 🟡 [F-084](#f-084) — `routes/verify.ts:120`

Severity 🟡 Medium ([CWE-347](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/347.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-084](#f-084) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker sends a JWT whose header sets `HS256`, HMAC-signed with the RSA public key, or alg none. `jwt.verify` runs with only the public key and no algorithms allowlist, so the header decides how the token is verified.
2. Here a verification that relies on the token's own header gates challenge evaluation.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: Juice Shop Express API - routes/verify.ts:120
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/verify.ts line 120
    App->>App: JWT verification accepts token-chosen algorithm
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-347
    alt Current state — F-084
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-084
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-179 — Pin RS256 in every JWT verification call
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-179](#m-179) (Pin `RS256` in every JWT verification call) lands, 🟡 [F-084](#f-084) — JWT verification accepts token-chosen algorithm is exploitable at `routes/verify.ts:120` (Medium-severity, [CWE-347](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/347.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-179](#m-179) (Pin `RS256` in every JWT verification call)

## 6. Security Architecture

This chapter is organized by security-control category. Findings are listed only where the affected control is described.

_Cataloged controls: 41 total - 4 adequate, 14 partial, 14 weak, 3 unsafe, 6 missing._

**How to read the verdicts.** Every control category (and every sub-control below it) carries exactly one status. The two red verdicts do **not** mean the same thing - this is the distinction that decides what you have to do about a finding:

| Status | Meaning | What to do |
|----------|------------------------------------|----------------------|
| 🔴 Unsafe | Present and relied upon, but defeated or<br/>trivially bypassable | Fix the existing control |
| 🔴 Missing | Control was never built | Add the control |
| 🟠 Weak | Present, but has exploitable gaps | Strengthen it |
| 🟡 Partial | Present, but with meaningful gaps | Close the gap |
| 🟢 Adequate | Control is present and sound | Nothing, keep it |

So "🔴 Unsafe" on a control category does *not* mean the control is absent - it means the control exists but does not hold (e.g. an `MD5` password hash, a raw-SQL query path, a hardcoded signing key). "🔴 Missing" is reserved for controls that were never built (e.g. no `Content-Security-Policy` header).

### 6.2 Identity and Authentication Controls

<a id="ctrl-identity-and-authentication-controls"></a>


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-003](#w-003), [W-012](#w-012), [W-013](#w-013)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe - registration accepts a client-supplied admin role, and the reset, change and OAuth flows each have exploitable gaps.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.2.1 OAuth implicit flow login](#621-oauth-implicit-flow-login)
- [6.2.2 Password change and credential handling](#622-password-change-and-credential-handling)
- [6.2.3 TOTP second factor](#623-totp-second-factor)
- [6.2.4 Password reset via security question](#624-password-reset-via-security-question)
- [6.2.5 User Registration](#625-user-registration)

**Implemented controls:** OAuth implicit flow login, Password change and credential handling, TOTP second factor, Password reset via security question, User Registration.

**Assessment:** Registration is the weakest flow: any caller can self-assign the admin role (🔴 [F-011](#f-011), 🟠 [F-075](#f-075), 🟠 [F-076](#f-076)). Password reset needs only a security answer (🟠 [F-026](#f-026)), password change can skip the current-password check (🟠 [F-015](#f-015)), and the OAuth adapter derives the local password from the email (🟠 [F-018](#f-018)). Each successful flow ends with the server issuing a session token, described in [§6.3 Session and Token Controls](#63-session-and-token-controls).

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — Chat identity taken from JWT decoded without signature
- 🟠 [F-015](#f-015) — Password change skips the current-password check
- 🟠 [F-016](#f-016) — OAuth login trusts userinfo profile without checking
- 🟠 [F-022](#f-022) — JWT verification does not pin the signing algorithm
- 🟠 [F-024](#f-024) — JWT verification does not pin the expected signing
- 🟠 [F-025](#f-025) — JWT verify call missing algorithms allowlist
- 🟠 [F-026](#f-026) — Unauthenticated password reset relies only on a security answer
- 🟠 [F-043](#f-043) — User passwords stored as unsalted MD5 hashes without a strength policy
- 🟠 [F-053](#f-053) — Passwords hashed with unsalted MD5
- 🟡 [F-081](#f-081) — Reset-password rate limit keyed on spoofable client address
- 🟡 [F-082](#f-082) — Unauthenticated review creation
- 🟡 [F-083](#f-083) — Chat identity taken from JWT decoded without signature
- 🟡 [F-084](#f-084) — JWT verification accepts token-chosen algorithm
- 🟡 [F-089](#f-089) — Wallet address claims accepted without authentication
- 🟡 [F-105](#f-105) — Unauthenticated Socket\.IO events alter challenge state and broadcast flags
- 🟡 [F-143](#f-143) — Unauthenticated WebSocket Channel

<a id="oauth-implicit-flow-login"></a>
#### 6.2.1 OAuth implicit flow login

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - implicit flow without a `state` parameter, and the local password is derivable from the email.

`login.component.ts` starts Google sign-in with `response_type=token`. `oauth.component.ts` reads the access token, fetches the userinfo profile, then registers and logs in a local account.

The diagram shows the frontend OAuth adapter ending in the local login:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as Angular OAuth Component
    participant G as Google
    participant API as Local Login API
    User->>G: Sign in with implicit flow
    G-->>SPA: Redirect with access token
    SPA->>G: Fetch userinfo profile
    SPA->>API: Register local account
    SPA->>API: Log in with derived password
    API-->>SPA: Session token
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Google OAuth uses response_type=token (implicit flow) with no state parameter. The access token is then sent in a URL query string to the userinfo endpoint. The redirect URI is checked against an allow-list from configuration, which is a partial mitigation. -->
Three weaknesses sit on this adapter:

- The implicit flow sends no `state` value, so login CSRF is possible (🟠 [F-017](#f-017)).
- The userinfo profile is trusted without further checks (🟠 [F-016](#f-016)).
- The local password is derived from the email, so anyone who knows an address can compute it (🟠 [F-018](#f-018)).
- The redirect URI allow-list from configuration is a partial mitigation only.



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="password-change-and-credential-handling"></a>
#### 6.2.2 Password change and credential handling

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - credentials travel in a GET query string and the current-password check can be skipped.

`routes/changePassword.ts` is registered as a GET route. It reads `current`, `new` and `repeat` from the query string, compares the current password hash, then updates the user record.

The diagram shows the intended password-change path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Change Password Route
    participant DB as Users Table
    User->>API: GET change-password with current, new and repeat
    API->>API: Resolve user from bearer token
    API->>DB: Compare hash of current password
    API->>DB: Update stored password
    API-->>User: Updated user
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Current and new passwords are sent in a GET query string, so they can end up in logs and history. The login component also contains hardcoded test credentials. The login password validator only requires a non-empty value. -->
Four weaknesses affect password change and credential handling:

- The comparison is skipped when `current` is absent (🟠 [F-015](#f-015)).
- Passwords in the query string end up in access logs and browser history (🟠 [F-049](#f-049), 🟡 [F-138](#f-138)).
- No strength policy applies beyond a non-empty value (🟡 [F-085](#f-085)).
- Test credentials are hardcoded in the login component (🟡 [F-145](#f-145)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="totp-second-factor"></a>
#### 6.2.3 TOTP second factor

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - the code check and temp-token type check run server-side, but attempt limiting was not evidenced and secrets are stored in plaintext.

`routes/2fa.ts` verifies a submitted TOTP code against the secret stored in the user model, after login returns a temporary token.

The diagram shows the TOTP verification step after the password login:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as 2FA Route
    participant DB as Users Table
    User->>API: Submit TOTP code with temp token
    API->>API: Check temp token type
    API->>DB: Load TOTP secret
    API->>API: Verify code
    API-->>User: Session token
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The temp token type is checked and the TOTP code is verified server-side. No attempt limiting was seen in the inspected code. The TOTP secret is stored in the user model. -->
Three weaknesses surround the second factor:

- TOTP secrets are stored in plaintext in the user table (🟡 [F-148](#f-148)).
- The password reset response leaks the TOTP secret (🟠 [F-057](#f-057)).
- The 2FA token sits in script-readable browser storage (🟠 [F-056](#f-056) — Bearer and 2FA tokens stored in localStorage and a script-readable cookie (Bearer and 2FA tokens stored in `localStorage` and a script-readable cookie)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="password-reset-via-security-question"></a>
#### 6.2.4 Password reset via security question

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - a knowledge-based security answer is the only factor and the endpoint needs no authentication.

`routes/resetPassword.ts` takes an email, a security answer and a new password. It compares the answer through an HMAC, then updates the user.

The diagram shows the intended reset path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Reset Password Route
    participant DB as Users and Answers
    User->>API: Email, security answer, new password
    API->>DB: Load stored answer HMAC
    API->>API: Compare HMAC of submitted answer
    API->>DB: Update password
    API-->>User: Updated user
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Password reset relies only on a knowledge-based security answer compared via a hardcoded-key HMAC. There is no rate limiting or out-of-band verification in the inspected code. The reset endpoint requires no authentication. -->
The reset path has no second channel:

- A single security answer authorizes the reset (🟠 [F-026](#f-026)).
- The rate limit is keyed on a spoofable client address (🟡 [F-081](#f-081)).
- Security answers can be created for arbitrary user IDs without authentication (🟠 [F-077](#f-077)).
- The question lookup reveals which emails are registered (🟡 [F-129](#f-129)).
- No strength policy applies to the new password (🟡 [F-092](#f-092)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="user-registration"></a>
#### 6.2.5 User Registration

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - `POST /api/Users` accepts a client-supplied admin role.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): Present at POST /api/Users -->
`POST /api/Users` is served by a generated `finale-rest` User resource. `server.ts` trims the password and checks only that it is non-empty before the record is created.

The diagram shows the intended registration path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Express API
    participant DB as Users Table
    User->>API: POST /api/Users with email and password
    API->>API: Trim and check password is non-empty
    API->>DB: Create user record
    API-->>User: Created user
```

**Security assessment**

Registration trusts the request body:

- The `role` field is bound from the body, so a caller can register as admin (🔴 [F-011](#f-011), 🟠 [F-075](#f-075), 🟠 [F-076](#f-076)).
- No password strength policy applies (🟡 [F-085](#f-085), 🟡 [F-092](#f-092)).
- Other privileged fields are also writable through the generic resource (🟠 [F-068](#f-068)).



**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Mass assignment of admin role during user registration
- 🟠 [F-068](#f-068) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware
- 🟠 [F-075](#f-075) — User registration accepts privileged role from request body
- 🟠 [F-076](#f-076) — Self-registration accepts a client-supplied admin role
- 🟡 [F-085](#f-085) — No password strength policy on registration
- 🟡 [F-091](#f-091) — Server trusts client-asserted challenge
- 🟡 [F-092](#f-092) — No password strength policy on registration
- 🟡 [F-129](#f-129) — Security question lookup reveals registered
- 🟡 [F-158](#f-158) — Regex with leading wildcard on unbounded

### 6.3 Session and Token Controls

<a id="ctrl-session-and-token-controls"></a>

**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak - the signing key is public in source, verification does not pin an algorithm, and tokens live in script-readable storage.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.3.1 Cookie/JWT authentication state and cookie signing](#631-cookiejwt-authentication-state-and-cookie-signing)
- [6.3.2 Client-side session token storage](#632-client-side-session-token-storage)
- [6.3.3 Session Token Issuance](#633-session-token-issuance)

**Implemented controls:** Cookie/JWT authentication state and cookie signing, Client-side session token storage, Session Token Issuance (JWT Based).

**Assessment:** This application uses one locally-signed `RS256` JWT for every session, regardless of the [§6.2](#62-identity-and-authentication-controls) flow that created it, plus an in-memory token map keyed by that token. The sub-sections trace cookie signing, browser storage and issuance. Main gaps: the embedded signing key (🟠 [F-019](#f-019)), unpinned verification algorithms (🟠 [F-022](#f-022), 🟠 [F-024](#f-024), 🟠 [F-025](#f-025)) and tokens that never leave the cache (🟡 [F-094](#f-094), 🟡 [F-095](#f-095)).

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🟠 [F-056](#f-056) — Bearer and 2FA tokens stored in localStorage and a script-readable cookie
- 🟡 [F-086](#f-086) — Password change trusts cached tokens
- 🟡 [F-093](#f-093) — Token checks
- 🟡 [F-094](#f-094) — Server-side token map sessions never expire or get
- 🟡 [F-095](#f-095) — Cached session tokens are trusted after their expiry
- 🟡 [F-150](#f-150) — Session token cookie lacks HttpOnly and Secure flags

<a id="cookiejwt-authentication-state-and-cookie-signing"></a>
#### 6.3.1 Cookie/JWT authentication state and cookie signing

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the cookie secret is a hardcoded literal and session state sits in an in-memory map with no eviction.

`cookieParser` signs cookies with a literal secret. `lib/insecurity.ts` looks up authenticated users in an in-memory map keyed by the cookie token.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): cookieParser uses a hardcoded literal secret 'kekse'. Authenticated users are looked up from an in-memory map keyed by the cookie token. Token validation internals in lib/insecurity.ts were not inspected. -->
Two problems follow from this design:

- Cached tokens are trusted after their expiry and never evicted (🟡 [F-094](#f-094), 🟡 [F-095](#f-095)).
- Several role helpers decode tokens without comparing expiry, issuer or audience (🟡 [F-093](#f-093)).
- Password change trusts the cached token without a lifetime check (🟡 [F-086](#f-086)).

Token validation internals were not inspected beyond these findings.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="client-side-session-token-storage"></a>
#### 6.3.2 Client-side session token storage

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the token sits in `localStorage` and a script-set cookie, readable by any XSS.

`login.component.ts` stores the bearer token in `localStorage` and in a cookie set through `cookieService`. `request.interceptor.ts` reads it for the `Authorization` header.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The bearer token is stored in localStorage and in a script-set cookie, with no HttpOnly protection. Any XSS can read and steal it. The 8-hour expiry is set client-side only. -->
Neither store has `HttpOnly` protection:

- Bearer and 2FA tokens are readable by script (🟠 [F-056](#f-056) — Bearer and 2FA tokens stored in localStorage and a script-readable cookie (Bearer and 2FA tokens stored in `localStorage` and a script-readable cookie)).
- The server-set session cookie lacks `HttpOnly` and `Secure` (🟡 [F-150](#f-150)).
- The stored-XSS findings make theft reachable (🟠 [F-037](#f-037), 🟠 [F-039](#f-039)).
- The 8-hour expiry is set client-side only.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="session-token-issuance"></a><a id="session-token-issuance-jwt-based"></a>
#### 6.3.3 Session Token Issuance

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - `RS256` and a 6-hour expiry are in place, but the signing key is public and nothing revokes tokens.

`lib/insecurity.ts` signs a token with `RS256` and a 6-hour expiry at login. `express-jwt` verifies it against the public key on protected routes.

The diagram shows token issuance and validation on a protected request:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Login Route
    participant JWT as JWT Signer
    participant MW as express-jwt Middleware
    User->>API: Valid credentials
    API->>JWT: Sign claims with RS256 private key
    API-->>User: Token and cookie
    User->>MW: Request with Authorization header
    MW->>MW: Verify token with public key
    MW-->>User: Route response
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Tokens are signed RS256 with a 6h expiry, and express-jwt verifies them against the public key. However, the signing key is embedded in source, and the token map has no revocation or logout handling in the inspected code. The cookie set at line 192 has no httpOnly or secure flags. -->
Four gaps weaken an otherwise sound `RS256` choice:

- The private key is embedded in source, so anyone with the code can mint tokens (🟠 [F-019](#f-019)).
- Verification omits an algorithm allowlist (🟠 [F-022](#f-022), 🟠 [F-024](#f-024), 🟠 [F-025](#f-025)).
- The token map has no revocation or logout handling (🟡 [F-094](#f-094)).
- The session cookie is set without `httpOnly` or `secure` (🟡 [F-150](#f-150)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.4 Authorization Controls

<a id="ctrl-authorization-controls"></a>


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-002](#w-002), [W-005](#w-005), [W-009](#w-009), [W-023](#w-023), [W-024](#w-024), [W-025](#w-025)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak - route guards are inconsistent and object ownership is largely unchecked.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.4.1 Route-level authorization middleware](#641-route-level-authorization-middleware)
- [6.4.2 Per-user ownership check in chatbot order lookup tool](#642-per-user-ownership-check-in-chatbot-order-lookup-tool)
- [6.4.3 Wallet ownership verification for NFT/wallet endpoints](#643-wallet-ownership-verification-for-nftwallet-endpoints)
- [6.4.4 Client-side bearer attachment for API calls](#644-client-side-bearer-attachment-for-api-calls)
- [6.4.5 Role checks and user binding](#645-role-checks-and-user-binding)

**Implemented controls:** Route-level authorization middleware, Per-user ownership check in chatbot order lookup tool, Wallet ownership verification for NFT/wallet endpoints, Client-side bearer attachment for API calls, Role checks and user binding.

**Assessment:** Authorization is applied per route with `isAuthorized()` and a few role helpers, not through a shared policy. Coverage gaps leave catalog writes (🟠 [F-071](#f-071), 🟠 [F-072](#f-072)) and sensitive routes (🟠 [F-068](#f-068)) open, and ownership is missing on baskets, feedback and reviews (🟠 [F-001](#f-001), 🔴 [F-008](#f-008), 🟡 [F-165](#f-165), 🟡 [F-166](#f-166)). The missing piece is server-side ownership checks as a pattern.

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🟠 [F-001](#f-001) — Missing object ownership checks on basket, basket item, checkout and review
- 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — Insecure Direct Object Reference
- 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Mass assignment of admin role during user registration

- 🟠 [F-071](#f-071) — Product catalog writable without role authorization
- 🟠 [F-072](#f-072) — Unauthenticated callers can modify any product including its

- 🟠 [F-076](#f-076) — Self-registration accepts a client-supplied admin role

<a id="route-level-authorization-middleware"></a>
#### 6.4.1 Route-level authorization middleware

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - guards cover many routes, but `PUT /api/Products` and several others have none.

`server.ts` registers `isAuthorized()`, `denyAll()` and `isAccounting()` per route across baskets, users, complaints and admin endpoints.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): isAuthorized/denyAll/isAccounting are applied to many baskets, users, complaints and admin routes, but coverage is inconsistent: PUT /api/Products is commented out, and several routes (/rest/chat, /rest/products/:id/reviews PUT, /rest/user/data-export, wallet, deluxe-membership, order-history, /rest/memories) have no route-level authorization middleware at registration. Per-handler checks were not inspected. -->
Coverage is inconsistent:

- The product update guard is commented out, so callers can modify products (🟠 [F-072](#f-072)).
- Sensitive routes are registered with no authentication middleware (🟠 [F-068](#f-068)).
- `isAuthorized()` checks only the signature, so any signed-in user can create products or list accounts (🟠 [F-061](#f-061), 🟠 [F-069](#f-069)).
- Recycle records are readable without authentication (🟠 [F-063](#f-063)).

Per-handler checks were not inspected.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="per-user-ownership-check-in-chatbot-order-lookup-tool"></a>
#### 6.4.2 Per-user ownership check in chatbot order lookup tool

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the lossy vowel-masked email makes ownership matching ambiguous.

`getOrderById` in `routes/chat.ts` requires an authenticated user and compares the order email with a vowel-masked form of the caller's email.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): getOrderById requires an authenticated user and compares the order email to a vowel-masked email. The masking is lossy (many distinct addresses collide), so ownership matching can be ambiguous. -->
Masking collapses many addresses into one value:

- The chatbot binds order ownership to that masked email (🟡 [F-163](#f-163)).
- Order export and history use the same lossy key instead of a user ID (🟡 [F-140](#f-140)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="wallet-ownership-verification-for-nftwallet-endpoints"></a>
#### 6.4.3 Wallet ownership verification for NFT/wallet endpoints

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - wallet addresses are taken from the request body with no proof of ownership.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): routes/nftMint.ts:41-47; routes/web3Wallet.ts:15-16 -->
`routes/nftMint.ts` and `routes/web3Wallet.ts` read `walletAddress` from the request body and act on it directly.

The diagram shows the intended wallet flow, which carries no ownership proof:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Client
    participant API as Wallet Route
    participant Web3 as Wallet Service
    Client->>API: POST walletAddress
    API->>Web3: Process address
    Web3-->>API: Result
    API-->>Client: Response
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Wallet addresses are taken directly from req.body.walletAddress with no signature or proof of ownership, and no authentication is visible in these handlers. Any client can claim any address. Authentication in route middleware was not inspected here. -->
Any client can claim any address:

- No signature or proof of ownership is required (🟡 [F-089](#f-089)).
- No authentication is visible in these handlers, and route middleware was not inspected here.



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="client-side-bearer-attachment-for-api-calls"></a>
#### 6.4.4 Client-side bearer attachment for API calls

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - the interceptor attaches the token and enforcement stays on the server.

`request.interceptor.ts` adds the bearer token to API calls, so services send only object IDs without per-call credentials.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Services call the API without per-call credentials, so authentication relies on a central interceptor (request.interceptor.ts) that was not read here. Authorization is enforced server-side, and the client only sends object IDs. Client route guards are UX only. -->
The client holds no authorization logic:

- Angular route guards are UX only, so server checks carry all enforcement.
- Because the token comes from browser storage, it is exposed as described in 🟠 [F-056](#f-056) — Bearer and 2FA tokens stored in localStorage and a script-readable cookie (Bearer and 2FA tokens stored in `localStorage` and a script-readable cookie).
- Client-asserted values such as `UserId` are trusted by the server in places (🟡 [F-087](#f-087)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="role-checks-and-user-binding"></a>
#### 6.4.5 Role checks and user binding

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - the accounting check and user binding work, but role checks cover only a few helpers.

`isAccounting()` verifies the JWT and checks the role claim. `appendUserId` binds `UserId` from the server-side token map instead of the request body.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Accounting access verifies the JWT and checks the role claim. appendUserId binds UserId from the server-side token map rather than the request body. Role checks are limited to a few helpers. -->
Binding applies on some routes only:

- Feedback and privacy requests accept a client-supplied owner (🟡 [F-087](#f-087), 🟡 [F-113](#f-113), 🟡 [F-169](#f-169)).
- Role checks exist for few operations, which leaves most routes at authentication only.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.5 Query Construction and Data Access Controls

<a id="ctrl-query-construction-and-data-access-controls"></a>


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-001](#w-001)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe - raw SQL and unvalidated NoSQL selectors are built from request data.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.5.1 Sequelize ORM database connection configuration](#651-sequelize-orm-database-connection-configuration)
- [6.5.2 Embedded MarsDB collection access control](#652-embedded-marsdb-collection-access-control)
- [6.5.3 Login query construction](#653-login-query-construction)

**Implemented controls:** Sequelize ORM database connection configuration, Login query construction.

**Assessment:** Sequelize backs most data access, but the login and search routes use raw SQL (🔴 [F-009](#f-009), 🔴 [F-002](#f-002)), and MarsDB selectors take request-controlled structure (🟠 [F-034](#f-034) (Route id interpolated into MarsDB \$where predicates), 🟠 [F-035](#f-035) — NoSQL operator injection in review update and like selectors, 🟠 [F-055](#f-055) (NoSQL \$where injection in order tracking), 🟡 [F-157](#f-157) (Attacker-controlled \$where JavaScript blocks)). The embedded SQLite file has no database-level authentication.

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🔴 [F-002](#f-002) — SQL injection in product search query
- 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — SQL injection in login query enables authentication bypass
- 🟠 [F-034](#f-034) — Route id interpolated into MarsDB \$where predicates
- 🟠 [F-035](#f-035) — NoSQL operator injection in review update and like selectors
- 🟠 [F-055](#f-055) — NoSQL \$where injection in order tracking
- 🟡 [F-157](#f-157) — Attacker-controlled \$where JavaScript blocks

<a id="sequelize-orm-database-connection-configuration"></a>
#### 6.5.1 Sequelize ORM database connection configuration

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - placeholder credentials and a plain file, with no database-level authentication or encryption.

<!-- Model implementation note (facts for the intro): models/index.ts createSequelize(): dialect sqlite, storage data/juiceshop.sqlite, retry on SQLITE_BUSY (max 5), IMMEDIATE transactions, logging disabled. -->
`createSequelize()` in `models/index.ts` opens SQLite at `data/juiceshop.sqlite`, retries `SQLITE_BUSY` up to five times, uses IMMEDIATE transactions and disables logging.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The embedded SQLite database is opened with hard-coded placeholder credentials ('username'/'password'), which SQLite ignores. The data file is a fixed relative path (data/juiceshop.sqlite). Access control therefore rests only on the application process and file-system permissions, and no database-level authentication or encryption is configured in this file. Per-query parameterization and the model setters were not inspected in the supplied slice, so no rating is given for them. -->
Access rests on the process and file permissions:

- The `username` and `password` values are placeholders that SQLite ignores.
- The path is fixed and relative, with no encryption configured (🟡 [F-144](#f-144)).
- Per-query parameterization was not inspected in this file, so it is not rated here.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="embedded-marsdb-collection-access-control"></a>
#### 6.5.2 Embedded MarsDB collection access control

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - collections are plain in-process stores with no access wrapper.

This control is not evidenced in the repository.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The reviews ('posts') and orders collections are created as plain in-process MarsDB collections with no authentication, schema validation or access wrapper at this definition. Any code in the application process can read and write them, so access control and query sanitisation depend entirely on callers. Only the collection definitions were inspected; caller-side controls were not. -->
The reviews and orders collections have no authentication, schema validation or wrapper at definition:

- Callers alone decide query safety, and selectors accept request-controlled structure (🟠 [F-035](#f-035), 🟠 [F-034](#f-034) — Route id interpolated into MarsDB \$where predicates (Route id interpolated into MarsDB \$where predicates)).
- `$where` JavaScript blocks are reachable from requests (🟠 [F-055](#f-055) — NoSQL \$where injection in order tracking (NoSQL \$where injection in order tracking), 🟡 [F-157](#f-157) — Attacker-controlled \$where JavaScript blocks (Attacker-controlled \$where JavaScript blocks)).

Caller-side controls were not inspected beyond these findings.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="login-query-construction"></a>
#### 6.5.3 Login query construction

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - the email is interpolated into raw SQL, so injection bypasses authentication.

`routes/login.ts` builds the credential lookup as a raw SQL string. The password is hashed before interpolation, but the email is inserted as submitted.

The diagram shows the intended login query path:
```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Login Route
    participant DB as SQLite Users Table
    User->>API: Email and password
    API->>API: Hash password
    API->>DB: Raw SQL lookup by email and hash
    DB-->>API: Matching user
    API-->>User: Session token
```

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The login query interpolates req.body.email directly into raw SQL, so the credential check can be bypassed by injection. The password is hashed before interpolation, but the email is not. -->
Two independent weaknesses sit on this query:

- The unescaped email lets an attacker alter the WHERE clause and sign in without a password (🔴 [F-009](#f-009)).
- The hash compared in the query is unsalted `MD5` (🟠 [F-043](#f-043) — User passwords stored as unsalted MD5 hashes without a strength policy (User passwords stored as unsalted `MD5` hashes without a strength policy)).



**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.6 Input Boundary Validation Controls

<a id="ctrl-input-boundary-validation-controls"></a>

**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe - the redirect allowlist uses substring matching and several inputs have no validation.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.6.1 Validation Approach](#661-validation-approach)
- [6.6.2 Chatbot tool input and policy enforcement](#662-chatbot-tool-input-and-policy-enforcement)
- [6.6.3 Validation of wallet address input](#663-validation-of-wallet-address-input)
- [6.6.4 WebSocket message payload validation](#664-websocket-message-payload-validation)
- [6.6.5 Guest basket input sanitization](#665-guest-basket-input-sanitization)
- [6.6.6 Redirect allowlist](#666-redirect-allowlist)

**Implemented controls:** Chatbot tool input and policy enforcement, WebSocket message payload validation, Guest basket input sanitization, Redirect allowlist.

**Assessment:** Validation is written per route. Guest basket items and WebSocket payloads get some checks, but wallet top-up amounts (🟠 [F-028](#f-028)), the coupon policy (🟠 [F-059](#f-059)) and wallet addresses (🟡 [F-155](#f-155)) do not. Memory photo uploads have no size limit (🟡 [F-156](#f-156)).

**Findings in this category without a control:**

- 🟠 [F-028](#f-028) — Wallet top-up amount accepted from client
- 🟠 [F-059](#f-059) — Model-selected coupon tool enforces discount policy only
- 🟡 [F-104](#f-104) — Stored profile image URL injected into CSP header
- 🟡 [F-151](#f-151) — B2B order evaluation blocks the event loop per request
- 🟡 [F-152](#f-152) — Unbounded synchronous parsing of uploaded XML
- 🟡 [F-154](#f-154) — Unbounded LLM consumption on unauthenticated chat endpoint
- 🟡 [F-155](#f-155) — Unbounded in-memory wallet address set fed
- 🟡 [F-156](#f-156) — Memory photo upload has no size limit and trusts client MIME

<a id="validation-approach"></a>
#### 6.6.1 Validation Approach

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - checks are hand-written per route, with no shared schema layer.

Chat tools declare `zod` schemas, WebSocket handlers use a regex, and registration only trims the password and checks it is non-empty.

**Security assessment**

Per-route checks leave several inputs open:

- The wallet top-up amount is accepted from the client (🟠 [F-028](#f-028)).
- A stored profile image URL reaches a CSP header (🟡 [F-104](#f-104)).
- Parsed XML and event-loop work are unbounded (🟡 [F-151](#f-151), 🟡 [F-152](#f-152)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="chatbot-tool-input-and-policy-enforcement"></a>
#### 6.6.2 Chatbot tool input and policy enforcement

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - the coupon limit exists only in prompt text, with no approval step.

`routes/chat.ts` exposes tools to the model, including `generateCoupon` and `getProductReviews`, each with a `zod` schema.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The coupon policy (max 10%, verified order) exists only in the system prompt. The generateCoupon tool schema accepts any number and execute does not enforce the limit, so a model-directed action is gated only by prompt text. No approval step is present. getProductReviews builds a \$where string from Number(id), which limits injection to numeric values (NaN is possible), though this is not a robust query construction. Authorization checks beyond the inspected tools were not examined. -->
Policy sits in the system prompt, not in code:

- The `generateCoupon` schema accepts any number, and `execute` does not enforce the 10% limit (🟠 [F-059](#f-059), 🟠 [F-073](#f-073) — Chatbot generateCoupon tool issues arbitrary discounts (Chatbot `generateCoupon` tool issues arbitrary discounts)).
- Prompt injection can steer the model into issuing discounts (🟠 [F-078](#f-078)).
- The `getProductReviews` `$where` string is built from `Number(id)`, which limits injection to numeric values but is not robust query construction.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="validation-of-wallet-address-input"></a>
#### 6.6.3 Validation of wallet address input

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - the wallet address gets no type, format or size check.

This control is not evidenced in the repository.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The request body walletAddress is added to an in-memory set without type, format or size checks, and the set is unbounded. Repeated requests can grow memory. -->
The request body value is added to an in-memory set unchecked:

- The set is unbounded, so repeated requests grow memory (🟡 [F-155](#f-155)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="websocket-message-payload-validation"></a>
#### 6.6.4 WebSocket message payload validation

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - payloads are typed as `any` and validated ad hoc.

`registerWebsocketEvents.ts` handles `Socket.IO` events with a regex and an `Array.isArray` check.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Handlers take payloads typed as any. Validation is ad hoc: a regex plus an Array.isArray check. 'notification received' lets any client remove a notification by flag, so any client can mutate shared server state. The code has no rate limiting or size limits. -->
Validation is thin on every handler:

- Any client can remove a notification by flag, mutating shared state (🟡 [F-091](#f-091), 🟡 [F-105](#f-105) — Unauthenticated Socket.IO events alter challenge state and broadcast flags (Unauthenticated Socket\.IO events alter challenge state and broadcast flags)).
- A leading-wildcard regex runs on any client string (🟡 [F-158](#f-158)).
- The code has no rate or size limits.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="guest-basket-input-sanitization"></a>
#### 6.6.5 Guest basket input sanitization

**Status:** 🟢 Adequate - 🟢 Adequate - items are parsed and validated before use.

Guest basket data from `sessionStorage` is parsed in a `try/catch`. Each item needs an integer product ID and a positive quantity.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): Guest basket data read from sessionStorage is parsed in try/catch and each item is validated (integer product ID, positive quantity) before use. This is a narrow client-side control, and the server must still validate. -->
This is a narrow client-side check:

- The server must still validate basket contents, since the client check can be bypassed.
- No finding is routed to this control.

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

<a id="redirect-allowlist"></a>
#### 6.6.6 Redirect allowlist

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - substring matching lets a URL that embeds an allowed URL pass.

The redirect route compares the target against an allow-list of URLs using `url.includes`.

**Security assessment**

<!-- Model assessment (facts the narrative must not contradict): The redirect allowlist uses substring matching (url.includes), so an attacker-controlled URL that embeds an allowed URL passes the check. -->
The allowlist check is defeated by construction:

- Any attacker-controlled URL containing an allowed URL passes, which yields an open redirect (🟡 [F-090](#f-090)).

**Relevant findings**

- None linked to this control; see the other findings of this category.

### 6.9 Cryptography Secrets and Data Protection


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-050](#w-050)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak - hardcoded keys, unsalted `MD5` and plaintext sensitive data.

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.9.1 Alchemy API key handling for WebSocket RPC](#691-alchemy-api-key-handling-for-websocket-rpc)
